Before that, I guess I had low points, but I can’t really say there was another stretch that was the same as this.\n\nDoctor: When your husband was injured, did you witness it happen?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really have a clear memory of seeing the actual moment it happened, if that’s what you mean. Like, I know he was injured, and I know what happened around it, and I’ve gone over it in my head so many times that sometimes it almost feels like I can picture pieces of it, but I don’t want to say I witnessed it if I didn’t actually see it with my own eyes.\n\nIt’s kind of all tangled together now — what I was told, what I imagined, what I remember from afterward. So… no, I don’t think I can say I witnessed the injury happen directly. At least not in a way I’m confident about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually high, energized, or like you needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, I think — like feeling wired or on top of the world, or not needing sleep and still having all this energy — but I can’t clearly point to a time like that.\n\nThere have been times I’ve stayed up late because I couldn’t settle, or because my mind wouldn’t shut off, but that doesn’t feel like what you’re describing exactly. It’s not like I was energized in a good way or suddenly doing a million things and feeling amazing. At least, not that I can remember.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed anything like being unusually high or needing much less sleep in that way. But I’m also second-guessing myself, because sometimes when you ask it like that, I start wondering, “Well, what counts as unusual?” But no, nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories or nightmares about your husband’s injury that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them memories exactly. That’s part of what gets confusing for me. I do think about it sometimes, and it can come up when I don’t really want it to, but I’m not sure it’s like a flashback or anything like that. It’s more like my mind circles around it, and then I start questioning what I actually remember versus what I’ve filled in.\n\nAs far as nightmares… I can’t really say I have clear nightmares about the injury itself. I mean, I’ve had bad dreams before, but I don’t wake up thinking, “That was the accident,” or seeing it all over again in some exact way. At least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t think I have unwanted memories or nightmares about it in the way you might be asking, but it does sit in my head sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. Before this past year… I mean, I’ve had low periods, sure, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks, I start trying to measure it like, “Was it every day? Was it two full weeks? Was I just stressed or tired?” And then I get kind of stuck.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a specific stretch where I can say, yes, that was definitely two weeks straight of being depressed nearly every day. It may have happened in some way, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m just filling in the blanks.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I don’t know. Nothing specific is coming to mind from before this past year that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress from your husband’s injury still going on in your life now?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s still going on. It’s been… about a year and five months now, I think, since my husband hurt his back and became mostly stuck at home. I don’t know, sometimes I lose track because it all kind of blends together, but it’s definitely not over.\n\nIt’s not like it was just an accident and then things went back to normal. His injury changed the way things run every day, and that stress is still just part of the house now. So yes, it’s ongoing — it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a day that feels a little lighter, is it usually because something about your husband’s situation eases up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can connect it that directly. I mean… sometimes if something with him is calmer that day, maybe the house feels a little less tight, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, today feels lighter because his situation eased up.”\n\nSome days feel a little better and I can’t really tell why. It might be the mood in the house, or maybe I slept differently, or maybe I just got through the morning without as much pressure building up. And other days, even if nothing new is happening with him, I still feel weighed down by it, like it’s just there in the background.\n\nSo I guess… maybe sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to say that’s usually the reason. I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nDoctor: When something genuinely good happens, does your mood lift, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I think it depends what you mean by “lift.” If something good happens, I can recognize that it’s good, like I know I should feel glad about it, and sometimes there is a little… I don’t know, a little break in the heaviness maybe.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it my whole mood lifting. Sometimes it’s more like I smile or I say, “That’s good,” but inside it still feels kind of flat or weighed down. Other times maybe I do feel lighter for a short bit, but then it fades pretty fast and I’m back to the same place.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is maybe briefly sometimes, but I haven’t really paid close enough attention to say it clearly. It’s not like I reliably bounce up when something good happens.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when nothing stressful is happening with your husband, do you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wish I could say it only happens when something with him is bad that day, because then at least it would make sense, like there’s a direct reason right in front of me. But even on days when there isn’t some new crisis or he’s not needing as much or the house is quieter, I still feel it.\n\nFor most of the time since all this started, it’s just been there. The guilt is there, like I’m failing somehow or not doing enough. The exhaustion is there even when the day itself isn’t especially stressful. I still don’t really feel interested in things the way I used to, even if there’s technically time or space to do something. And… the thoughts about death, those have been there too. Not every single second, and some weeks are less crushing than others, but they don’t just disappear because that particular day is calmer.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing big is happening with my husband, I still feel depressed. It’s more like the stress made everything worse, but the depression doesn’t turn off just because the stress is quiet for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die as punishment for what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… that’s hard to say out loud. Part of me knows, or at least I’m supposed to know, that nobody should be thinking of death as some kind of punishment. But when I’m really down in it, yes, it feels like that. It feels like maybe it would be fair, like if I didn’t wake up, that would be what I deserve for what I’ve done to my family and my crew.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in, because those thoughts keep coming back. It’s not just “I’m tired” in a normal way. It’s more like I keep thinking it would be better if I didn’t wake up, like everyone would be spared me or the damage I’ve caused. And then I feel ashamed even saying that, because I know how awful that sounds.\n\nI don’t know if I believe it every minute, but when the guilt gets really loud, dying feels like the punishment that would match it. Like that would settle the account somehow. And I know that probably doesn’t make sense from the outside, but inside it can feel very real.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you lost interest or pleasure in most things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past year… no, not like this. I mean, I’ve had tired stretches before, like anybody, where I didn’t feel like doing much for a few days, or I’d skip something because work ran me into the ground. But I still had things that felt like mine.\n\nI used to paint. Not professionally or anything, but I’d get lost in it for hours. And I liked cooking big meals, especially if people were coming over or even just making too much food because it felt good to make something. I used to run before jobs sometimes, even if it was early and I complained about it, because it cleared my head. And theater rehearsals… I’d show up for those. I cared about being there, about the lines and the people and the whole thing.\n\nNow all of that feels like chores from somebody else’s life. Like I can remember that I used to care, but I don’t feel connected to it. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or don’t cook, there’s not even much disappointment, just this blankness, like, “Okay, I didn’t do it.” So before this past year, I don’t think I had a two-week period where most things lost their pleasure like that. This feels different.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you actually caused your husband’s injury?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that in a clean way. If you mean, do I know for a fact that I directly caused it, like with my own hands or because I chose one exact thing that made it happen… I don’t know. I can’t prove that. But inside, it feels like yes. It feels like I’m the reason he got worse.\n\nI keep going back over everything, every decision, every job, every time I pushed or didn’t push, every time I was distracted or tired or thought something would be fine. And it all turns into this one conclusion in my head, that if I had been different, better, more careful, less selfish, then maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt or maybe he wouldn’t have gotten worse after. It’s like I infected the whole situation somehow.\n\nAnd it’s not just him. I keep thinking the crew lost work because of me, and the clients’ homes got messed up because of me, and everything I touched turned bad. Like I poisoned it. That’s the phrase that keeps coming up in my head, like I poisoned everything I touched. So when you ask if I believe I caused his injury… I guess I believe I caused the damage around it, even if I can’t explain it in a way that would make sense to someone else.\n\nAnd then it feels like whatever happens to me after that, I deserve it. Like if people are angry, if things fall apart, if I lose everything, that’s just the consequence of what I did. It feels hard to separate what actually happened from what I feel responsible for, because the guilt just swallows the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe, as a fact, that you poisoned everything you touched?\n\nPatient: As a fact… I mean, I know how that sounds. If you’re asking me like, could someone test it or prove it, no, I don’t think there’s some literal poison on my hands or anything like that. I know that doesn’t make sense in that kind of way.\n\nBut it feels true. That’s the problem. It feels like everything I was near got ruined because of me. My husband got worse, and I keep thinking there had to be something I did or didn’t do that made that happen. The crew lost work, and I hear myself saying, “That’s because of me.” Clients’ homes got messed up, and even if there are a hundred other reasons something went wrong, my mind goes straight to, “You touched it, you were involved, so of course it went bad.”\n\nSo do I believe it as a fact? I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean “fact.” But when I’m sitting alone with it, it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like the truth about me, like I damage things by being part of them. And then whatever happens to me after that feels deserved. Like if everything falls apart, that’s just what should happen because I caused it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It feels like the truth about me, like I damage things by being part of them. And then whatever happens to me after that feels deserved. Like if everything falls apart, that’s just what should happen because I caused it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas anyone else told you that you are not actually to blame for what happened, but you still feel completely certain that you are?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / carpet_floor_or_tile_installer_or_finisher / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep thinking I'm the reason my husband got worse, the crew lost work, and clients' homes got messed up, like I poisoned everything I touched and deserve whatever happens to me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. People have said that to me. My husband has, in his way, and other people too, like they’ll say, “You couldn’t have known,” or “That wasn’t on you,” or “There were other reasons things went wrong.” They try to explain it like it was circumstances, or bad luck, or decisions other people made, not something I caused.

And I hear them. I can repeat the words back. I understand, in my head, what they’re trying to tell me. But it doesn’t land. It’s like there’s this wall between what they say and what I feel is true.

Because I still keep coming back to the same thing: my husband got worse, the crew lost work, clients’ homes got messed up, and I was right there in the middle of it. I was part of it. So my mind says, “That was you. You poisoned it. Everything you touched went bad.”

Even when someone says, “You’re not to blame,” I don’t feel relieved. I almost feel like they just don’t know the whole truth, or they’re being kind because they don’t want to say what I already know. And then I think, if all that damage came from me, then whatever happens to me after is just what I deserve. So yes… people tell me I’m not actually to blame, but I still feel like I am. Completely.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.23, F31.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I’m scared of where my thoughts have been going. I keep thinking it would be better if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just go to sleep and not have to open my eyes again, that would be easier for everybody.\n\nAnd it’s not just being tired, you know? It feels heavier than that. Sometimes I get this feeling like dying would be the fair punishment for what I’ve done to my family and my crew. Like I’ve caused too much damage or let people down too badly, and there should be some kind of consequence for that. I know that probably sounds awful to say out loud, but it’s been in my head a lot, and I didn’t know what else to do.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic exactly, but… my husband got injured, and a few months after that is when this really settled in. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, at least not for me emotionally. It was more like things kept piling up, and then a few months later I realized I wasn’t coming back up out of it.\n\nSo… the depression, this heaviness, has been with me for about a year and two months now. I keep thinking back and trying to pinpoint it, because I know people ask, like, “When did it start?” And the best I can say is it started a few months after his injury and it just… stayed. It didn’t lift the way I thought it might.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another time in your life when you went through a period like this depression?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve had hard times before, everybody has, and I’ve been sad or stressed before. Work stuff, family stuff, money stuff, the usual things that can wear you down.\n\nBut if you mean like this — where it just stays and everything feels this heavy for this long — I don’t know. I don’t think I can point to another time that was exactly like this. I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry, because at the time you don’t always label it as “a period” or anything. You just keep getting up and doing what you have to do.\n\nThis past year feels different to me, though. It’s like it took root and didn’t let up. Before that, I guess I had low points, but I can’t really say there was another stretch that was the same as this.\n\nDoctor: When your husband was injured, did you witness it happen?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really have a clear memory of seeing the actual moment it happened, if that’s what you mean. Like, I know he was injured, and I know what happened around it, and I’ve gone over it in my head so many times that sometimes it almost feels like I can picture pieces of it, but I don’t want to say I witnessed it if I didn’t actually see it with my own eyes.\n\nIt’s kind of all tangled together now — what I was told, what I imagined, what I remember from afterward. So… no, I don’t think I can say I witnessed the injury happen directly. At least not in a way I’m confident about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually high, energized, or like you needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, I think — like feeling wired or on top of the world, or not needing sleep and still having all this energy — but I can’t clearly point to a time like that.\n\nThere have been times I’ve stayed up late because I couldn’t settle, or because my mind wouldn’t shut off, but that doesn’t feel like what you’re describing exactly. It’s not like I was energized in a good way or suddenly doing a million things and feeling amazing. At least, not that I can remember.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed anything like being unusually high or needing much less sleep in that way. But I’m also second-guessing myself, because sometimes when you ask it like that, I start wondering, “Well, what counts as unusual?” But no, nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories or nightmares about your husband’s injury that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them memories exactly. That’s part of what gets confusing for me. I do think about it sometimes, and it can come up when I don’t really want it to, but I’m not sure it’s like a flashback or anything like that. It’s more like my mind circles around it, and then I start questioning what I actually remember versus what I’ve filled in.\n\nAs far as nightmares… I can’t really say I have clear nightmares about the injury itself. I mean, I’ve had bad dreams before, but I don’t wake up thinking, “That was the accident,” or seeing it all over again in some exact way. At least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t think I have unwanted memories or nightmares about it in the way you might be asking, but it does sit in my head sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. Before this past year… I mean, I’ve had low periods, sure, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks, I start trying to measure it like, “Was it every day? Was it two full weeks? Was I just stressed or tired?” And then I get kind of stuck.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a specific stretch where I can say, yes, that was definitely two weeks straight of being depressed nearly every day. It may have happened in some way, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m just filling in the blanks.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I don’t know. Nothing specific is coming to mind from before this past year that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress from your husband’s injury still going on in your life now?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s still going on. It’s been… about a year and five months now, I think, since my husband hurt his back and became mostly stuck at home. I don’t know, sometimes I lose track because it all kind of blends together, but it’s definitely not over.\n\nIt’s not like it was just an accident and then things went back to normal. His injury changed the way things run every day, and that stress is still just part of the house now. So yes, it’s ongoing — it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a day that feels a little lighter, is it usually because something about your husband’s situation eases up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can connect it that directly. I mean… sometimes if something with him is calmer that day, maybe the house feels a little less tight, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, today feels lighter because his situation eased up.”\n\nSome days feel a little better and I can’t really tell why. It might be the mood in the house, or maybe I slept differently, or maybe I just got through the morning without as much pressure building up. And other days, even if nothing new is happening with him, I still feel weighed down by it, like it’s just there in the background.\n\nSo I guess… maybe sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to say that’s usually the reason. I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nDoctor: When something genuinely good happens, does your mood lift, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I think it depends what you mean by “lift.” If something good happens, I can recognize that it’s good, like I know I should feel glad about it, and sometimes there is a little… I don’t know, a little break in the heaviness maybe.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it my whole mood lifting. Sometimes it’s more like I smile or I say, “That’s good,” but inside it still feels kind of flat or weighed down. Other times maybe I do feel lighter for a short bit, but then it fades pretty fast and I’m back to the same place.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is maybe briefly sometimes, but I haven’t really paid close enough attention to say it clearly. It’s not like I reliably bounce up when something good happens.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when nothing stressful is happening with your husband, do you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wish I could say it only happens when something with him is bad that day, because then at least it would make sense, like there’s a direct reason right in front of me. But even on days when there isn’t some new crisis or he’s not needing as much or the house is quieter, I still feel it.\n\nFor most of the time since all this started, it’s just been there. The guilt is there, like I’m failing somehow or not doing enough. The exhaustion is there even when the day itself isn’t especially stressful. I still don’t really feel interested in things the way I used to, even if there’s technically time or space to do something. And… the thoughts about death, those have been there too. Not every single second, and some weeks are less crushing than others, but they don’t just disappear because that particular day is calmer.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing big is happening with my husband, I still feel depressed. It’s more like the stress made everything worse, but the depression doesn’t turn off just because the stress is quiet for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die as punishment for what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… that’s hard to say out loud. Part of me knows, or at least I’m supposed to know, that nobody should be thinking of death as some kind of punishment. But when I’m really down in it, yes, it feels like that. It feels like maybe it would be fair, like if I didn’t wake up, that would be what I deserve for what I’ve done to my family and my crew.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in, because those thoughts keep coming back. It’s not just “I’m tired” in a normal way. It’s more like I keep thinking it would be better if I didn’t wake up, like everyone would be spared me or the damage I’ve caused. And then I feel ashamed even saying that, because I know how awful that sounds.\n\nI don’t know if I believe it every minute, but when the guilt gets really loud, dying feels like the punishment that would match it. Like that would settle the account somehow. And I know that probably doesn’t make sense from the outside, but inside it can feel very real.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you lost interest or pleasure in most things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past year… no, not like this. I mean, I’ve had tired stretches before, like anybody, where I didn’t feel like doing much for a few days, or I’d skip something because work ran me into the ground. But I still had things that felt like mine.\n\nI used to paint. Not professionally or anything, but I’d get lost in it for hours. And I liked cooking big meals, especially if people were coming over or even just making too much food because it felt good to make something. I used to run before jobs sometimes, even if it was early and I complained about it, because it cleared my head. And theater rehearsals… I’d show up for those. I cared about being there, about the lines and the people and the whole thing.\n\nNow all of that feels like chores from somebody else’s life. Like I can remember that I used to care, but I don’t feel connected to it. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or don’t cook, there’s not even much disappointment, just this blankness, like, “Okay, I didn’t do it.” So before this past year, I don’t think I had a two-week period where most things lost their pleasure like that. This feels different.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you actually caused your husband’s injury?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that in a clean way. If you mean, do I know for a fact that I directly caused it, like with my own hands or because I chose one exact thing that made it happen… I don’t know. I can’t prove that. But inside, it feels like yes. It feels like I’m the reason he got worse.\n\nI keep going back over everything, every decision, every job, every time I pushed or didn’t push, every time I was distracted or tired or thought something would be fine. And it all turns into this one conclusion in my head, that if I had been different, better, more careful, less selfish, then maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt or maybe he wouldn’t have gotten worse after. It’s like I infected the whole situation somehow.\n\nAnd it’s not just him. I keep thinking the crew lost work because of me, and the clients’ homes got messed up because of me, and everything I touched turned bad. Like I poisoned it. That’s the phrase that keeps coming up in my head, like I poisoned everything I touched. So when you ask if I believe I caused his injury… I guess I believe I caused the damage around it, even if I can’t explain it in a way that would make sense to someone else.\n\nAnd then it feels like whatever happens to me after that, I deserve it. Like if people are angry, if things fall apart, if I lose everything, that’s just the consequence of what I did. It feels hard to separate what actually happened from what I feel responsible for, because the guilt just swallows the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe, as a fact, that you poisoned everything you touched?\n\nPatient: As a fact… I mean, I know how that sounds. If you’re asking me like, could someone test it or prove it, no, I don’t think there’s some literal poison on my hands or anything like that. I know that doesn’t make sense in that kind of way.\n\nBut it feels true. That’s the problem. It feels like everything I was near got ruined because of me. My husband got worse, and I keep thinking there had to be something I did or didn’t do that made that happen. The crew lost work, and I hear myself saying, “That’s because of me.” Clients’ homes got messed up, and even if there are a hundred other reasons something went wrong, my mind goes straight to, “You touched it, you were involved, so of course it went bad.”\n\nSo do I believe it as a fact? I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean “fact.” But when I’m sitting alone with it, it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like the truth about me, like I damage things by being part of them. And then whatever happens to me after that feels deserved. Like if everything falls apart, that’s just what should happen because I caused it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else told you that you are not actually to blame for what happened, but you still feel completely certain that you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. People have said that to me. My husband has, in his way, and other people too, like they’ll say, “You couldn’t have known,” or “That wasn’t on you,” or “There were other reasons things went wrong.” They try to explain it like it was circumstances, or bad luck, or decisions other people made, not something I caused.\n\nAnd I hear them. I can repeat the words back. I understand, in my head, what they’re trying to tell me. But it doesn’t land. It’s like there’s this wall between what they say and what I feel is true.\n\nBecause I still keep coming back to the same thing: my husband got worse, the crew lost work, clients’ homes got messed up, and I was right there in the middle of it. I was part of it. So my mind says, “That was you. You poisoned it. Everything you touched went bad.”\n\nEven when someone says, “You’re not to blame,” I don’t feel relieved. I almost feel like they just don’t know the whole truth, or they’re being kind because they don’t want to say what I already know. And then I think, if all that damage came from me, then whatever happens to me after is just what I deserve. So yes… people tell me I’m not actually to blame, but I still feel like I am. Completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But even on days when there isn’t some new crisis or he’s not needing as much or the house is quieter, I still feel it.\n\nFor most of the time since all this started, it’s just been there. The guilt is there, like I’m failing somehow or not doing enough. The exhaustion is there even when the day itself isn’t especially stressful. I still don’t really feel interested in things the way I used to, even if there’s technically time or space to do something. And… the thoughts about death, those have been there too. Not every single second, and some weeks are less crushing than others, but they don’t just disappear because that particular day is calmer.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing big is happening with my husband, I still feel depressed. It’s more like the stress made everything worse, but the depression doesn’t turn off just because the stress is quiet for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die as punishment for what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… that’s hard to say out loud. Part of me knows, or at least I’m supposed to know, that nobody should be thinking of death as some kind of punishment. But when I’m really down in it, yes, it feels like that. It feels like maybe it would be fair, like if I didn’t wake up, that would be what I deserve for what I’ve done to my family and my crew.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in, because those thoughts keep coming back. It’s not just “I’m tired” in a normal way. It’s more like I keep thinking it would be better if I didn’t wake up, like everyone would be spared me or the damage I’ve caused. And then I feel ashamed even saying that, because I know how awful that sounds.\n\nI don’t know if I believe it every minute, but when the guilt gets really loud, dying feels like the punishment that would match it. Like that would settle the account somehow. And I know that probably doesn’t make sense from the outside, but inside it can feel very real.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you lost interest or pleasure in most things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past year… no, not like this. I mean, I’ve had tired stretches before, like anybody, where I didn’t feel like doing much for a few days, or I’d skip something because work ran me into the ground. But I still had things that felt like mine.\n\nI used to paint. Not professionally or anything, but I’d get lost in it for hours. And I liked cooking big meals, especially if people were coming over or even just making too much food because it felt good to make something. I used to run before jobs sometimes, even if it was early and I complained about it, because it cleared my head. And theater rehearsals… I’d show up for those. I cared about being there, about the lines and the people and the whole thing.\n\nNow all of that feels like chores from somebody else’s life. Like I can remember that I used to care, but I don’t feel connected to it. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or don’t cook, there’s not even much disappointment, just this blankness, like, “Okay, I didn’t do it.” So before this past year, I don’t think I had a two-week period where most things lost their pleasure like that. This feels different.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you actually caused your husband’s injury?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that in a clean way. If you mean, do I know for a fact that I directly caused it, like with my own hands or because I chose one exact thing that made it happen… I don’t know. I can’t prove that. But inside, it feels like yes. It feels like I’m the reason he got worse.\n\nI keep going back over everything, every decision, every job, every time I pushed or didn’t push, every time I was distracted or tired or thought something would be fine. And it all turns into this one conclusion in my head, that if I had been different, better, more careful, less selfish, then maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt or maybe he wouldn’t have gotten worse after. It’s like I infected the whole situation somehow.\n\nAnd it’s not just him. I keep thinking the crew lost work because of me, and the clients’ homes got messed up because of me, and everything I touched turned bad. Like I poisoned it. That’s the phrase that keeps coming up in my head, like I poisoned everything I touched. So when you ask if I believe I caused his injury… I guess I believe I caused the damage around it, even if I can’t explain it in a way that would make sense to someone else.\n\nAnd then it feels like whatever happens to me after that, I deserve it. Like if people are angry, if things fall apart, if I lose everything, that’s just the consequence of what I did. It feels hard to separate what actually happened from what I feel responsible for, because the guilt just swallows the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe, as a fact, that you poisoned everything you touched?\n\nPatient: As a fact… I mean, I know how that sounds. If you’re asking me like, could someone test it or prove it, no, I don’t think there’s some literal poison on my hands or anything like that. I know that doesn’t make sense in that kind of way.\n\nBut it feels true. That’s the problem. It feels like everything I was near got ruined because of me. My husband got worse, and I keep thinking there had to be something I did or didn’t do that made that happen. The crew lost work, and I hear myself saying, “That’s because of me.” Clients’ homes got messed up, and even if there are a hundred other reasons something went wrong, my mind goes straight to, “You touched it, you were involved, so of course it went bad.”\n\nSo do I believe it as a fact? I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean “fact.” But when I’m sitting alone with it, it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like the truth about me, like I damage things by being part of them. And then whatever happens to me after that feels deserved. Like if everything falls apart, that’s just what should happen because I caused it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else told you that you are not actually to blame for what happened, but you still feel completely certain that you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. People have said that to me. My husband has, in his way, and other people too, like they’ll say, “You couldn’t have known,” or “That wasn’t on you,” or “There were other reasons things went wrong.” They try to explain it like it was circumstances, or bad luck, or decisions other people made, not something I caused.\n\nAnd I hear them. I can repeat the words back. I understand, in my head, what they’re trying to tell me. But it doesn’t land. It’s like there’s this wall between what they say and what I feel is true.\n\nBecause I still keep coming back to the same thing: my husband got worse, the crew lost work, clients’ homes got messed up, and I was right there in the middle of it. I was part of it. So my mind says, “That was you. You poisoned it. Everything you touched went bad.”\n\nEven when someone says, “You’re not to blame,” I don’t feel relieved. I almost feel like they just don’t know the whole truth, or they’re being kind because they don’t want to say what I already know. And then I think, if all that damage came from me, then whatever happens to me after is just what I deserve. So yes… people tell me I’m not actually to blame, but I still feel like I am. Completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, that’s the part that scares me, because I know people keep telling me it wasn’t my fault. They say it wasn’t that simple, that there were other things going on, that I’m taking too much on myself. And I can hear them saying it, I really can. I don’t want to act like I don’t understand the words.

But inside, I feel certain. Completely certain, most of the time. Like if I trace it all back, it keeps ending with me. My husband got worse, and I keep thinking, “That happened because of me.” The crew lost work, and I think, “That was because I was involved.” Clients’ homes got messed up, and my mind just goes, “Of course they did, because you touched it.”

It’s like there’s this stain on me, or like I poisoned everything I got near. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I don’t just feel sad about it — I feel guilty, like I caused damage and now whatever happens to me is deserved. Even when someone tries to reassure me, it doesn’t really reach the part of me that already knows I’m the reason it all went bad.

It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, at least not for me emotionally. It was more like things kept piling up, and then a few months later I realized I wasn’t coming back up out of it.\n\nSo… the depression, this heaviness, has been with me for about a year and two months now. I keep thinking back and trying to pinpoint it, because I know people ask, like, “When did it start?” And the best I can say is it started a few months after his injury and it just… stayed. It didn’t lift the way I thought it might.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another time in your life when you went through a period like this depression?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve had hard times before, everybody has, and I’ve been sad or stressed before. Work stuff, family stuff, money stuff, the usual things that can wear you down.\n\nBut if you mean like this — where it just stays and everything feels this heavy for this long — I don’t know. I don’t think I can point to another time that was exactly like this. I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry, because at the time you don’t always label it as “a period” or anything. You just keep getting up and doing what you have to do.\n\nThis past year feels different to me, though. It’s like it took root and didn’t let up. Before that, I guess I had low points, but I can’t really say there was another stretch that was the same as this.\n\nDoctor: When your husband was injured, did you witness it happen?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really have a clear memory of seeing the actual moment it happened, if that’s what you mean. Like, I know he was injured, and I know what happened around it, and I’ve gone over it in my head so many times that sometimes it almost feels like I can picture pieces of it, but I don’t want to say I witnessed it if I didn’t actually see it with my own eyes.\n\nIt’s kind of all tangled together now — what I was told, what I imagined, what I remember from afterward. So… no, I don’t think I can say I witnessed the injury happen directly. At least not in a way I’m confident about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually high, energized, or like you needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, I think — like feeling wired or on top of the world, or not needing sleep and still having all this energy — but I can’t clearly point to a time like that.\n\nThere have been times I’ve stayed up late because I couldn’t settle, or because my mind wouldn’t shut off, but that doesn’t feel like what you’re describing exactly. It’s not like I was energized in a good way or suddenly doing a million things and feeling amazing. At least, not that I can remember.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed anything like being unusually high or needing much less sleep in that way. But I’m also second-guessing myself, because sometimes when you ask it like that, I start wondering, “Well, what counts as unusual?” But no, nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories or nightmares about your husband’s injury that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them memories exactly. That’s part of what gets confusing for me. I do think about it sometimes, and it can come up when I don’t really want it to, but I’m not sure it’s like a flashback or anything like that. It’s more like my mind circles around it, and then I start questioning what I actually remember versus what I’ve filled in.\n\nAs far as nightmares… I can’t really say I have clear nightmares about the injury itself. I mean, I’ve had bad dreams before, but I don’t wake up thinking, “That was the accident,” or seeing it all over again in some exact way. At least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t think I have unwanted memories or nightmares about it in the way you might be asking, but it does sit in my head sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. Before this past year… I mean, I’ve had low periods, sure, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks, I start trying to measure it like, “Was it every day? Was it two full weeks? Was I just stressed or tired?” And then I get kind of stuck.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a specific stretch where I can say, yes, that was definitely two weeks straight of being depressed nearly every day. It may have happened in some way, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m just filling in the blanks.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I don’t know. Nothing specific is coming to mind from before this past year that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress from your husband’s injury still going on in your life now?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s still going on. It’s been… about a year and five months now, I think, since my husband hurt his back and became mostly stuck at home. I don’t know, sometimes I lose track because it all kind of blends together, but it’s definitely not over.\n\nIt’s not like it was just an accident and then things went back to normal. His injury changed the way things run every day, and that stress is still just part of the house now. So yes, it’s ongoing — it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a day that feels a little lighter, is it usually because something about your husband’s situation eases up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can connect it that directly. I mean… sometimes if something with him is calmer that day, maybe the house feels a little less tight, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, today feels lighter because his situation eased up.”\n\nSome days feel a little better and I can’t really tell why. It might be the mood in the house, or maybe I slept differently, or maybe I just got through the morning without as much pressure building up. And other days, even if nothing new is happening with him, I still feel weighed down by it, like it’s just there in the background.\n\nSo I guess… maybe sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to say that’s usually the reason. I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nDoctor: When something genuinely good happens, does your mood lift, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I think it depends what you mean by “lift.” If something good happens, I can recognize that it’s good, like I know I should feel glad about it, and sometimes there is a little… I don’t know, a little break in the heaviness maybe.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call it my whole mood lifting. Sometimes it’s more like I smile or I say, “That’s good,” but inside it still feels kind of flat or weighed down. Other times maybe I do feel lighter for a short bit, but then it fades pretty fast and I’m back to the same place.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is maybe briefly sometimes, but I haven’t really paid close enough attention to say it clearly. It’s not like I reliably bounce up when something good happens.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when nothing stressful is happening with your husband, do you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wish I could say it only happens when something with him is bad that day, because then at least it would make sense, like there’s a direct reason right in front of me. But even on days when there isn’t some new crisis or he’s not needing as much or the house is quieter, I still feel it.\n\nFor most of the time since all this started, it’s just been there. The guilt is there, like I’m failing somehow or not doing enough. The exhaustion is there even when the day itself isn’t especially stressful. I still don’t really feel interested in things the way I used to, even if there’s technically time or space to do something. And… the thoughts about death, those have been there too. Not every single second, and some weeks are less crushing than others, but they don’t just disappear because that particular day is calmer.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing big is happening with my husband, I still feel depressed. It’s more like the stress made everything worse, but the depression doesn’t turn off just because the stress is quiet for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die as punishment for what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… that’s hard to say out loud. Part of me knows, or at least I’m supposed to know, that nobody should be thinking of death as some kind of punishment. But when I’m really down in it, yes, it feels like that. It feels like maybe it would be fair, like if I didn’t wake up, that would be what I deserve for what I’ve done to my family and my crew.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in, because those thoughts keep coming back. It’s not just “I’m tired” in a normal way. It’s more like I keep thinking it would be better if I didn’t wake up, like everyone would be spared me or the damage I’ve caused. And then I feel ashamed even saying that, because I know how awful that sounds.\n\nI don’t know if I believe it every minute, but when the guilt gets really loud, dying feels like the punishment that would match it. Like that would settle the account somehow. And I know that probably doesn’t make sense from the outside, but inside it can feel very real.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you lost interest or pleasure in most things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past year… no, not like this. I mean, I’ve had tired stretches before, like anybody, where I didn’t feel like doing much for a few days, or I’d skip something because work ran me into the ground. But I still had things that felt like mine.\n\nI used to paint. Not professionally or anything, but I’d get lost in it for hours. And I liked cooking big meals, especially if people were coming over or even just making too much food because it felt good to make something. I used to run before jobs sometimes, even if it was early and I complained about it, because it cleared my head. And theater rehearsals… I’d show up for those. I cared about being there, about the lines and the people and the whole thing.\n\nNow all of that feels like chores from somebody else’s life. Like I can remember that I used to care, but I don’t feel connected to it. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or don’t cook, there’s not even much disappointment, just this blankness, like, “Okay, I didn’t do it.” So before this past year, I don’t think I had a two-week period where most things lost their pleasure like that. This feels different.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you actually caused your husband’s injury?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that in a clean way. If you mean, do I know for a fact that I directly caused it, like with my own hands or because I chose one exact thing that made it happen… I don’t know. I can’t prove that. But inside, it feels like yes. It feels like I’m the reason he got worse.\n\nI keep going back over everything, every decision, every job, every time I pushed or didn’t push, every time I was distracted or tired or thought something would be fine. And it all turns into this one conclusion in my head, that if I had been different, better, more careful, less selfish, then maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt or maybe he wouldn’t have gotten worse after. It’s like I infected the whole situation somehow.\n\nAnd it’s not just him. I keep thinking the crew lost work because of me, and the clients’ homes got messed up because of me, and everything I touched turned bad. Like I poisoned it. That’s the phrase that keeps coming up in my head, like I poisoned everything I touched. So when you ask if I believe I caused his injury… I guess I believe I caused the damage around it, even if I can’t explain it in a way that would make sense to someone else.\n\nAnd then it feels like whatever happens to me after that, I deserve it. Like if people are angry, if things fall apart, if I lose everything, that’s just the consequence of what I did. It feels hard to separate what actually happened from what I feel responsible for, because the guilt just swallows the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe, as a fact, that you poisoned everything you touched?\n\nPatient: As a fact… I mean, I know how that sounds. If you’re asking me like, could someone test it or prove it, no, I don’t think there’s some literal poison on my hands or anything like that. I know that doesn’t make sense in that kind of way.\n\nBut it feels true. That’s the problem. It feels like everything I was near got ruined because of me. My husband got worse, and I keep thinking there had to be something I did or didn’t do that made that happen. The crew lost work, and I hear myself saying, “That’s because of me.” Clients’ homes got messed up, and even if there are a hundred other reasons something went wrong, my mind goes straight to, “You touched it, you were involved, so of course it went bad.”\n\nSo do I believe it as a fact? I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean “fact.” But when I’m sitting alone with it, it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like the truth about me, like I damage things by being part of them. And then whatever happens to me after that feels deserved. Like if everything falls apart, that’s just what should happen because I caused it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else told you that you are not actually to blame for what happened, but you still feel completely certain that you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. People have said that to me. My husband has, in his way, and other people too, like they’ll say, “You couldn’t have known,” or “That wasn’t on you,” or “There were other reasons things went wrong.” They try to explain it like it was circumstances, or bad luck, or decisions other people made, not something I caused.\n\nAnd I hear them. I can repeat the words back. I understand, in my head, what they’re trying to tell me. But it doesn’t land. It’s like there’s this wall between what they say and what I feel is true.\n\nBecause I still keep coming back to the same thing: my husband got worse, the crew lost work, clients’ homes got messed up, and I was right there in the middle of it. I was part of it. So my mind says, “That was you. You poisoned it. Everything you touched went bad.”\n\nEven when someone says, “You’re not to blame,” I don’t feel relieved. I almost feel like they just don’t know the whole truth, or they’re being kind because they don’t want to say what I already know. And then I think, if all that damage came from me, then whatever happens to me after is just what I deserve. So yes… people tell me I’m not actually to blame, but I still feel like I am. Completely.\n\nDoctor: Are you completely certain that what happened was your fault, even though other people tell you it wasn’t?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s the part that scares me, because I know people keep telling me it wasn’t my fault. They say it wasn’t that simple, that there were other things going on, that I’m taking too much on myself. And I can hear them saying it, I really can. I don’t want to act like I don’t understand the words.\n\nBut inside, I feel certain. Completely certain, most of the time. Like if I trace it all back, it keeps ending with me. My husband got worse, and I keep thinking, “That happened because of me.” The crew lost work, and I think, “That was because I was involved.” Clients’ homes got messed up, and my mind just goes, “Of course they did, because you touched it.”\n\nIt’s like there’s this stain on me, or like I poisoned everything I got near. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I don’t just feel sad about it — I feel guilty, like I caused damage and now whatever happens to me is deserved. Even when someone tries to reassure me, it doesn’t really reach the part of me that already knows I’m the reason it all went bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If you’re asking me like, could someone test it or prove it, no, I don’t think there’s some literal poison on my hands or anything like that. I know that doesn’t make sense in that kind of way.\n\nBut it feels true. That’s the problem. It feels like everything I was near got ruined because of me. My husband got worse, and I keep thinking there had to be something I did or didn’t do that made that happen. The crew lost work, and I hear myself saying, “That’s because of me.” Clients’ homes got messed up, and even if there are a hundred other reasons something went wrong, my mind goes straight to, “You touched it, you were involved, so of course it went bad.”\n\nSo do I believe it as a fact? It feels like the truth about me, like I damage things by being part of them. And then whatever happens to me after that feels deserved. Like if everything falls apart, that’s just what should happen because I caused it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else told you that you are not actually to blame for what happened, but you still feel completely certain that you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. I can repeat the words back. I understand, in my head, what they’re trying to tell me. But it doesn’t land. It’s like there’s this wall between what they say and what I feel is true.\n\nBecause I still keep coming back to the same thing: my husband got worse, the crew lost work, clients’ homes got messed up, and I was right there in the middle of it. I almost feel like they just don’t know the whole truth, or they’re being kind because they don’t want to say what I already know. And then I think, if all that damage came from me, then whatever happens to me after is just what I deserve. So yes… people tell me I’m not actually to blame, but I still feel like I am. Completely.\n\nDoctor: Are you completely certain that what happened was your fault, even though other people tell you it wasn’t?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s the part that scares me, because I know people keep telling me it wasn’t my fault. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I have been, and I hate saying that because it makes me sound… I don’t know. But yes.

It’s usually a man’s voice. Not like my own thoughts exactly, not like when you remember something someone said. It sounds like it’s coming from behind me, clear enough that I turn around. It’s happened at job sites, when I’m working or looking over something, and it’s happened in my kitchen too, when I’m just standing there doing something normal.

He’ll say things like, “You missed it,” or, “Everyone knows.” Short things. Not a whole conversation most of the time, but it cuts through everything, like someone is standing right behind my shoulder. And I’ll turn around expecting to see a guy there, or someone from the crew, or my husband, or just… someone. But nobody’s there.

And then I feel stupid and scared at the same time, because it was so clear. It isn’t like a vague feeling. It’s not just worry. I hear it like sound, like a voice in the room. Sometimes I’ll check the hallway or look around the kitchen because I’m sure someone must have said it. But there’s no one.
